# Course Transcript — Reference Material

Raw lecture transcript for the Udemy course *"Use Claude Cowork, Claude Code,
Claude Design with 70+ Resources, 200+ Prompts, 20+ Claude Skills, 10+ Claude
Plugins."* Kept as the source-of-truth reference behind
[Course Overview](./course-overview) and the
[Master Study Guide](./claude-masterclass-study-guide).

:::note
This is an auto-generated lecture transcript, lightly reformatted (section/
lecture headers converted to Markdown, caption index numbers stripped). It
was not manually edited for typos — auto-captioning occasionally renders
**"Claude" as "Cloud"** (e.g. "cloud co-work" = "Claude Cowork", "cloud code"
= "Claude Code"). Read it with that substitution in mind, or use the
[Master Study Guide](./claude-masterclass-study-guide) for a cleaned-up,
synthesized version of this same material.
:::

### Introduction to Git & GitHub


All right, before we go any further with Cloud Code, I think it's a good idea to do a quick
intro to GitHub, because this is one of those things that will save you a lot of headaches
down the road. So what actually is GitHub? In short, it's a place where you store your code
online. Because when you are building something locally on your machine, inside a code editor,
like Visual Studio Code, Curso, Antigravity, that code only lives on your computer.
So we store the code locally. But GitHub lets you push that code up to the cloud,
which means you can access it from anywhere, share it with others, and most importantly,
if something goes wrong, you can always get it back. Now the good news is, it's completely free,
so you don't need to pay for it. You would only start paying if you are managing a larger team,
and even that is a pretty small amount per person, because it's just for dollars a month.
So if you don't have an account yet, please go ahead and create a new account. In the top right
corner, just click sign up, provide all the details, and let's move on. Now once you are logged
in, let's cover a few key things you will see inside. The first very important one is a repository,
or repo for short. So this is basically just a folder in the cloud where you project lives.
Now how to create a new repo? You can click on this plus button, create new repo, new repository,
and now you can give it a name. So let's say test, and this is very important,
choose visibility. So you can set it to public, which means anyone can find it online and view
the code. Or you can change it to private, which means only you have access. Then just confirm,
create repository, and that's it. We successfully created our first repository. By the way,
this is one of my test accounts. Now when I go to one of the repos, for example this one,
the next thing worth understanding for now is branches. By default, every project, every repo
has a main branch. Alright? So think of this as the stable working version of your code,
of your repo. When you want to make changes or try something new, you create a separate branch.
And that separate branch is an exact copy of main branch. But anything you do in the separate branch,
so in the copy, doesn't affect the original branch, so the main branch. So if things go wrong,
you can just delete that branch and main is completely safe, is untouched. And once you
are happy with your changes, new features, some improvements, you can merge that branch back into
main branch and then delete it. And the way you merge a branch into main branch
is through something called a pull request.
So it basically shows you what you change between your branch and main. Alright?
So you can compare what's inside the branch you are working in and develop some,
let's say, features with Cloud Code and compare to the main branch.
Then when you click create pull request, green means something was added and red means something
was removed. So once everything looks good, there are no any errors, you can merge it.
Okay? Now if you are just starting out and working on your own, you don't have to worry too much
about branches and pull requests, because you can just commit directly to main while you are
learning, but it's not best practice long term, but it's completely fine to start with.
One last thing, if you find a public repo on GitHub that you want to explore or use,
you can download it to your machine using git clone command and then provide the URL
of this repo. And that's it. Or you can also fork. So cloning creates a local copy
of a repository on your computer. Now you can also fork the repo, so you can create a copy
of someone else's repository on your own GitHub account by clicking on this button.
Alright? And then create fork.
And that's really all you need to know for now. As we go through the course, everything will start
clicking. I just wanted to help you get familiar with GitHub. That's it for this lesson, and I'll see you later.


### Understanding IDEs


Welcome back.
So far, we have learned how to run cloud code in the terminal.
And that's great, but most people, especially when working on real projects, prefer to use
something called an IDE.
So let's talk about what that actually is.
IDE stands for Integrated Development Environment.
I know that sounds a bit technical, but it's really just three tools combined into a single app.
The first is File Explorer.
You can think of it like Finder on Mac or File Explorer on Windows, which is built right into the app.
So basically, you can browse all your folders and files visually.
You can click to open any file instantly and see your entire project's structure all in one place.
So it's really handy when your project starts to grow and you've got loads of files to keep track of.
Now, the second component is a text editor to read and write code.
And you can think of it like Notepad or Notes on your phone, but smarter and built specifically for code.
So it has syntax highlighting, which just means it colors different parts of your code
differently to make it much easier to read.
It lets you navigate large files very easily, and everything about it is designed specifically
for writing code.
And the third component is an AI chat window.
Think of it like Cloud.AI, ChargePT, Grok, DeepSeek, or any other LLM, but built directly
into where your code lives.
So you can basically ask questions without ever leaving your editor.
An AI sees your code inside in the context.
You can get help right where you work inside IDE, so you don't get to jump between different apps.
So really, it's just those three things combined.
So file explorer, text editor, and AI chat.
And that equals an IDE, so Integrated Development Environment.
Developers use IDEs to write code.
Now when it comes to which IDE to use, there are a few options worth knowing about, in my opinion.
All right, so the first and most established one is Visual Studio Code, or VS Code for short.
So it was built by Microsoft, it's been around the longest, and it's incredibly well supported. It's also free.
It works on Windows, Mac, and Linux, and there are thousands of extensions you can add to it.
It's one of the most developers learned on, and it's still one of the most popular tools
in the industry today. All right.
The second is Cursor.
And this is a new option that's built on top of VS Code, but with a much stronger focus on AI coding.
If you have heard people talking about vibe coding, just describing what you want and
letting AI build it, Cursor is one of the go-to tools for vibe coding.
In the next lesson, I will show you exactly how to get the Cloud Code set up inside Cursor,
if that's the IDE you want to go, and the third is Antigravity.
Think of this as VS Code 2.0.
It's also built on top of VS Code, so everything you know from there carries over, but it's
more modern, cleaner, and has an even bigger focus on AI than Cursor.
And some features are still fairly new, but overall, it's a really impressive tool.
Personally, I like both VS Code and Antigravity, so throughout this course, I will be showing
things in both tools.
In the next section, we'll start by setting up Cloud Code in Cursor, then we'll move on

### Exercise: Build a simple personal portfolio page with Claude Code

### How to Get Better Results from Claude Code


So now that you have built your first app, I want to share something that's going to make a huge
difference to the quality of what you build with Cloud Code. And something most beginners
completely overlook. So here is the thing. Cloud Code is only as good as the instructions you give
it. Because the model itself is incredibly powerful. Right now, we are at a point where
the models are so good that if you are getting poor results, 9 times out of 10, it's not
Cloud's fault. It's the input. So basically, the first rule is garbage in, garbage out.
Think about it this way. Imagine you just hired a really talented developer. And on their first day,
you say, build me an app. And they will just stare at you. They need to know what the app does,
what it's for, what features it needs, what it should look like, and how it should behave.
So the more clearly you can describe what you want, the better the result is going to be.
And Cloud Code is exactly the same. And here is something a lot of people miss.
It's a bit like client work. When a client comes to you, they will say one thing. But what they
actually want is something much more specific that they haven't fully thought through yet.
So it's your job to extract that. Same thing with Cloud Code. You need to think through what you
actually want before you start typing. Because Cloud can only work with what you give it.
Of course, you can use blank mode, but you need to have a rough idea. So here is the most important
mindset shift. Stop thinking about your project as a product and start thinking about it as a list
of features. Alright? So not products, features. Don't say build me an app. Break it into specific
features. Each one becomes a clear task. List every feature individually. Each feature equals
one clear task. Features together equals your product. A product is basically a list of features.
And this sounds simple, but it makes a massive difference. You see a lot of people describe a
product to Cloud and then get frustrated when the result is not what they imagined.
But the problem is they described a product, not features. And this mindset shift is especially
important when you build more complex and sophisticated apps, products, and systems.
And Cloud can't just magically read your mind. Let's say you want to build a booking app for a
small business. If you just tell Cloud, build me a booking app, it's going to make a lot of
assumptions. Some of them might be right, but some of them might be wrong. So instead, you need to say
something more specific. For example, I want a booking app with these four specific features.
A calendar view, an email confirmation system, a client logging page, maybe a payment screen.
So now Cloud has something clear to work with. And those features put together are your product.
And that's the way to think about it. You might hear people call this PRD, which stands for
Product Requirements Document. We also call it a plan or a to-do list. It doesn't really matter
what you call it. It's all the same thing. It's just a clear breakdown of what you want to build.
Of course, you can use Cloud Code to prepare it for you. But the point is, we need to spend some
time on planning using Cloud Code in order to get better results. And the more specific and detailed
the plan is, the better Cloud will do. And the next level of this is once Cloud builds each feature,
before moving on to the next one, ask it to test what it just built. You can just say,
now write me a quick test to make sure this feature works correctly before we move on.
Or just simply, let's test this feature to make sure everything is working properly.
So after each feature, ask Cloud to test it. Don't build on broken foundations. Test each feature
before next. Fix problems while they are small. And never stack untested code. Because you catch
problems early when they are easy to fix, rather than at the end when everything is tangled together.
So the simple rule is, think in features, all right? Be specific and test as you go.
Of course, at each step, at each stage, you can use Cloud Code to do it for you. But this is the
framework you can follow to build more sophisticated, more production-ready apps and systems.
Do that consistently, and you will get dramatically better results
than most people using Cloud Code right now.


### Git vs Github


Welcome back.
In one of the previous lessons, we already created our GitHub account.
Now let's talk about what Git and GitHub actually are and the differences, because a lot of
beginners get these two confused and it's worth taking a minute to clear it up.
Let's start with Git.
Git is a version control system.
You can think of it like the version history in Google Docs.
You can go back and see every edit that was ever made and what the document looked like
at any point in time.
And Git does exactly the same thing, but for your code.
So every time you save a version of your project, Git creates a snapshot of exactly what everything
looked like at that moment.
So if something goes wrong, clause, makes a mistake, you accidentally delete a file
or a new feature breaks everything, you can simply just go back to any previous snapshot
and recover your work.
I would say it's like having an unlimited undo button for your entire project.
And the thing is, the bigger your project gets, the more valuable this becomes.
Because the more complex your code is, the easier it is for things to break.
So Git is the tool on your computer.
It tracks every change you make, creates snapshots of your project, and you have unlimited undo for your code.
Now GitHub is a completely separate thing.
GitHub is an online platform, basically a website, where you store all of that version
history in the cloud.
As I showed you in one of the previous lessons.
So think of it this way, Git is the tool that runs on your computer and keeps track of all
your changes locally, while GitHub, on the other hand, is the place online where you
send a copy of everything, so it's safely backed up.
Even if your laptop breaks, gets stolen, or you accidentally delete your entire project
folder from your machine, everything is still sitting there on the cloud, on GitHub, in
your account, so you can get it back anytime.
It's also really useful if you ever want to work on the same project from different computers,
or collaborate with other people, because everyone can access the same code from GitHub.
So GitHub, as you have seen, is the website in the cloud.
You create an account to store your code online safely.
You can back up your work, your code, your projects, if your laptop breaks, and you can
access it from anywhere, from any device.
So a simple way to remember the difference, Git is the tool, and GitHub is the storage.
So Git tracks your changes on your computer, Cloud Code will make to your code, and GitHub
saves them online.
So they work together, but they are two completely different things.
Now the really good news is that for this course, you don't need to learn any Git commands
or understand how any of this works under the hood, because we are going to get Cloud
Code to handle all of it automatically.
So every time it makes changes to your project, it will save a version and push everything up to GitHub.
So you always have a full backup of your work, you know, even without having to think about it.
So before Cloud Code can do that, we need to do a quick one-time setup.
We need to install Git on your machine and connect it to the GitHub account you created earlier.
So let's do that now.

### The Claude.md File - Your Project's Memory


Alright, so there is something really important we need to talk about before we go any further.
And that's the CloudMD file. Let me explain.
Basically, every time you start a new Cloud Code session, it starts completely fresh. Alright?
So if you told it in a previous session to always commit your code to GitHub or to follow
certain rules when building your project, it won't remember any of that.
And you are basically starting from zero every single time.
And that's the problem we need to solve.
And that's where the Cloud.md file comes in.
This is a special file that gives Cloud persistent memory for your project.
So every time you start a new session, Cloud automatically reads the file first, and thanks
to that, it always knows the rules of your project.
What it should do, and what it shouldn't do, without you having to repeat yourself
every single time when you prompt it.
So for example, if I want Cloud to always commit my work to GitHub in every session,
I need to have that written in the Cloud.md file.
Otherwise, it's just going to forget.

### Understanding Sub-Agents in Claude Code


In this lesson, we are going to look at one of the most important concepts in Cloud Code
and that is sub-agents.
So whether you are a VIP coding or you are an experienced developer using Cloud Code
to speed up your workflow, understanding how sub-agents work is going to make a massive
difference in the quality of what we build.
So by the end of this lesson, you'll know exactly what sub-agents are, what built-in
agents are already available to you, how to create your own specialized agents, and most
importantly, why using sub-agents is so important.
All right, so before we jump into the demo, let me first explain what sub-agents actually
are, because I think it's really important to understand the concept before we start using them.
So what you are looking at here is how sub-agents work in Cloud Code, and the idea is actually really simple.
At the top of the slide, we have the main agent, and you can see it's labeled as the manager.
And this is the agent we have been chatting with this whole time.
It's your primary conversation where you type your prompts and get responses.
And if you look at three points next to it, we can see what its job is.
It coordinates all tasks, it stays clean and focused, and it collects results and summaries.
So the main agent is supposed to do all the heavy lifting itself.
Its job is really just to manage things.
Now if you look below the main agent, we can see these arrows pointing down to three
separate agents.
And these are our sub-agents.
On the left, we have the explorer agent, which searches the code base and finds files.
In the middle, we have the coder agent, which writes and edits code.
And on the right, we have the reviewer agent, which reviews code for quality and bugs.
Now these are just examples.
You can create whatever kinds of sub-agents you want, but this gives you a good idea of
how you can split up the work between different specialized agents.
And then if you look at those arrows pointing back to underneath, the sub-agents, we can
see it says returns short summary only.
And this is the key part.
When a sub-agent finishes its work, it doesn't dump everything it did back into the main conversation.
It just sends a short summary.
So instead of, let's say, 70,000 tokens worth of work landing in the main conversation,
you might only get a few hundred tokens of summary coming to the conversation.
And this is the most important takeaway.
Each sub-agent gets its own context window.
So none of their work fills up the main conversation.
And this is really what makes sub-agents so powerful, because each one works independently
in its own space.
And the main agent stays clean and focused the entire time.
And the other really powerful thing about this setup is that you can run multiple sub-agents
at the same time.
Just like we see on this slide with the three agents all running in parallel.
So instead of the main agent going through tasks one by one, you can have two or three
or even more sub-agents all working on different tasks at the same time.
And this makes everything much faster, and it keeps the main conversation clean.
So if you think about it, the main agent really does become like a project manager.
It's not doing the actual work itself.
It's just coordinating a team, telling each agent what to do and then collecting the results
when they are done.
And that's really the whole idea behind sub-agents.
Now let's see this in action.
I've got our financial tracker project open from one of the previous lessons.
So please open that project as well, or really any project you have been working on.
It doesn't matter which one, because what we are going to learn applies to every project.
So let's start by having a look at what sub-agents are already built into Cloud Code, because
you might not even realize that they are there.
So what I'm going to do is switch over to planning mode.
And then I'm going to ask the agent to show us what it's working with.
So I'm going to say, hey, please can you list all of the sub-agents that you currently have access to?
And set that off.
And what we can see is that Cloud actually comes with a few built-in sub-agents right out of the box.
So let's go through them one by one.
First up, we have the general purpose agent.
And this one is good for researching complex questions, searching through code, and execute
multi-step tasks.
It has access to all of the tools, which means it can do pretty much anything.
This is the one Cloud will use when it needs to do a broader search, and if it's not sure
exactly where to look.
Then we have the status line setup agent.
And this is the one that used to set up a little dashboard, a little dashboard bar,
that you might have seen at the bottom of Cloud Code.
It has access to read and edit tools, so it can make changes to your configuration.
Then you have explore agent.
And this is a really useful one, because it's specifically built for exploring your codebase.
So finding files by pattern, searching code for keywords, and answering questions about the codebase.
So about how a project is structured.
The important thing to notice is that it says read only, all right?
Which means it can look through everything, but it can't actually change any of your files.
So it's completely safe to use for investigation work.
Next we have the plan agent, which is a software architect agent.

It just analyzes and plans.
And finally, we have the Cloud Code guide agent.

It has access to things like web search and web fetch, so it can look up the latest documentation for you.

So on the left side of the slide, we can see what happens without sub-agents.
Every conversation in Cloud Code has a limit of 200,000 tokens.
And as we keep chatting with the agent, sending prompts, getting responses, having it read files and run commands,
we are filling up that context window more and more.
So all the work happens in one conversation and the context fills up fast.
At some point, usually around 70 to 80 percent, Cloud Code is going to start compacting the conversation.
And what compaction means is it takes everything that's happened so far and summarizes it down to save space.
And while that sounds good, the problem is that during this compaction, you lose a lot of important details.
Things like specific decisions that were made earlier, parts of the implementation plan or some technical choices.
So all of that can get dropped. All right.
So important details get lost and quality drops.
So once that context is gone, the agent starts making mistakes because it's forgotten things that are important.
So the main conversation ends up overloaded.
And this is actually the number one reason why people get frustrated with Cloud Code.
Because they will say that they tried it and the results were not good.
But what actually happened is they are trying to do everything in one long conversation with the main agent.
And the context window fills up, gets compacted, and then the quality of the code and of the Cloud responses drops.
Now on the right side, we can see how sub-agents fix this.
Because each agent gets its own context window.
So when we delegate a task to a sub-agent, like the explorer agent or our reviewer, that agent works
in its own completely separate space.
So it has its own context window.
And only short summaries return to the main conversation.
So instead of, let's say, 70,000 tokens worth of work hitting the main context, you might only get a
few hundred tokens of summary coming back.
And our main conversation stays protected, sitting at maybe 15%.
While the sub-agents are doing all the heavy lifting.
So the key takeaway is that each sub-agent gets its own context window.
So none of their work fills up the main conversation.
Now, to be honest, sub-agents are not there to reduce your overall token usage.
You are still going to use roughly the same number of tokens in total.
What sub-agents do is they protect your main conversation from filling up.
And that's what keeps the quality high throughout the entire build, entire project.

### Deploying Your Project to the Web


Welcome back! So up until now in this course everything we have built has been
run locally on your own machine and that's completely fine for building and
testing things out. But at some point we are going to want other people to
actually be able to see and use what you have built. Maybe it's a website for a
client or a project you want to show in your portfolio. Or maybe you just want to
share it with your friends to get some feedback. And in order to do that we need to
deploy it. You need to deploy your website, your application or your system. And that's
what this lesson is all about. Now before we actually do the deployment I want to
quickly explain what deployment actually means. Because it's one of those words
that sounds more complicated than it really is. So what we are looking at here
is the difference between building locally and deploying to the web. On the
left side we have local deployment. And this is what we have been doing
throughout the entire course. When you build something with cloud code the
files are stored on your computer. You can open the project in your browser
using something like localhost. But that only works on your machine. So nobody
else in the world can see it. It's just for you. And then on the right side we
have deployment. All deployment really means is taking those same files from
your computer and putting them on a server that's connected to the Internet.
Once they are on the server anyone with the URL can visit your project from
basically anywhere. So it's that simple. You are just moving your project from
your computer to the Internet. So when you run your project locally you can
only access it via localhost in browser. So your local server. Which is of course
great for building and testing. It's also private. So just for you. But when you
deploy it to the web anyone can visit it. And files live on an Internet server. So
anyone can access it via public URL anywhere. You can share it with clients,
friends, anyone. And it's public. So it is accessible for everyone. So the key point
is building is for you and deploying is for everyone else. Now there are several
different platforms out there that make deployment really easy. And most of them
have three tiers. So let me quickly show you the two most popular ones. The first
one is of course Vercel. So if you go to Vercel.com this is what their website
looks like. Vercel is really popular for deploying front-end websites and web

### What is an LLM and How AI Models Actually Work


Okay, in this video, really quickly, let's discuss what is an LLM model and how it works.
So how a models actually work, training data, tokens, context window, prompt and outputs.
No PhD required.
And there is a definition.
LLM, so large language model, is a neutral network trained on massive amounts of text.
It learns statistical patterns in language.
So when you give it a prompt, it predicts the most useful sequence of words to produce.
And now, large language model even doesn't understand what it writes.
I mean the text, okay?
It doesn't understand the text because it works on numbers and on data like this.
It doesn't understand text.
It doesn't look things up or for rules.
It predicts the most useful response based on patterns from its training.
And how the training works, trained on billions of texts, books, code, webpages, conversations.
Compresses everything into numerical patterns.
Happens once you use an already trained model.
Then what happens, it actually splits the text into tokens.
And each token is for characters.
So if we have a sentence, which is hello world, then it's gonna split it right here
because we've got H-E-L-L.
So these are four characters and it's one token.
And then it's gonna take this entire word and we've got two tokens.
And for example, we may have a page that is 500 tokens.
So context windows are measured in tokens, not words.
Let's move on to context window because it's the working memory.
Everything the model can see at once.
Prompt plus history.
Other messages drop off when the window fills up.
Bigger window, more context, higher cost.
And now let's talk about the prompt output.
You write a prompt.
It predicts the best continuation.
Not thinking, choosing the strategically strongest tokens.
Better prompts, better, more focused outputs.
And once again, it predicts the words that should be next.
So let's say you've got cat is sitting on.
And then it predicts, like, if the word should be on a table or on a plant.
Like plant doesn't make sense.
So probably it will have, like, let's say, like, 5% probability.
But table will have, like, 95%.
And then if you have in LLM models, you've got the temperature.
You can specify if the higher the temperature is, you can specify that it should be more,
I would say, vivid.
It should provide more vivid answers.
So more versatile.
So the answers will be kind of more creative.
However, if the temperature is low, it's going to be very predictable.
And actually, it works this way.
We won't go in depth in this course about LLMs, about every single detail, because it's
the course about cloth.
However, it's very important to understand that our LLM predicts the words that's going to be next.
So each word is predicted based on the data it has.
So if the pattern, let's say, like, we've got multiple different documents, if the pattern,
like, occurred across multiple different documents, and five out of six times we had, like, cat
is sitting on a table, it's going to give it a probability, and then it's going to be
probably used in our context.

### Prompt Engineering for Real Results


Writing good prompts inside Cloth is super important
because they directly reflect the results we receive.
So in this video, we are going to talk about
prompt engineering for real results.
And to begin with a quick introduction,
to create good prompts, we need to be really descriptive.
We need to describe a task as we would do this
to other human or for example, other friend,
or just to a child.
So it should be really easy to follow and understand.
And now we've got a core formula that contains a few elements
role, task, context, and format, and it equals result.
And these are the components that define a good prompt.
So for a role, it's a very nice strategy
to just provide the sentence at the beginning,
that for example, act as a professional marketer,
act as a salesperson, or as a content creator.
And then we just give Cloth the role, how to behave exactly.
So then it takes that role instead of just being
a general assistant for various tasks.
We've got a task, so we need to describe step by step
what we want to do.
And now more content better, but not every time,
like make sure to provide really clear instructions.
Don't just give all the information in really messy format,
just too much information, really unnecessary.
Just give everything you want to achieve
in the prompt in a clear way.
Just give some context.
So for example, you can give a file as a reference.
Let's say you write an article and you can give a file
with articles already written.
So it follows your specific style,
your writing tone, and so on.
Or for example, let's say in a chat,
you create, let's say maybe some title for a book,
you give already created titles,
so it exactly knows what's good and what's not.
Additionally, we've got a format.
So you need to specify if the output should be in a,
let's say in a written text in the file,
or even it should create an artifact.
So let's say interactive dashboard, okay?
So it's very important.
And now, this formula, you can combine all of these elements
and create the result,
or you can just pick, let's say some of them.
So let's say you want to pick a role and a task,
but without context. It's fine.
If you just run a simple requests,
you don't need to have a context for it.
Or if you want to just use cloth
to create something from scratch,
as it thinks that's the best,
also don't provide the context or the format.
So this is very important.
You can actually combine multiple different elements
or just use all of them.
We've got three patterns.
So the first one is role, task and format.
Gives cloth a clear persona, objective and output structure.
So for example, you're a product manager,
write a free bullet summary of this user feedback,
use plain English, no jargon.
The second technique is chain of thought.
So asks cloth to reason step-by-step
before giving a final answer. So for example,
walk me through your reasoning step-by-step,
then give me your final recommendation at the end.
So you do everything step-by-step.
Also, you can just ask cloth
to give me a step-by-step plan,
or let's say you create, I don't know, maybe a book.
You can firstly ask for chapters and specific,
and something, what should be in them,
and then just expand each one separately.
So it makes the entire process step-by-step.
Also, we can use it for complex decisions,
analysis, or anything that needs logic.
And the third one pattern is iterative prompting.
So you can start broad,
then refine with follow-up messages to improve the output.
For example, get a draft output, make it shorter.
Now make the tone more direct, add a CTA at the end.
And I would say iterative prompting
I use with every single request.
So constantly you teach cloth
how to basically behave in a chat,
or how to act, or how to create a response.
Of course, remember, you've got the skills
to create a recipe, how to perform a task.
So then you don't need to basically
even create the entire prompt for something,
because right away you create like a skill,
so a recipe of how to perform the task.
However, still, if you wanna run something from scratch,
just use prompt engineering.
And it's much better than just providing

let's provide something random and test our luck, okay?
Like with this, with just doing like all of the tasks
by this way, you literally just rely on your luck

you need to upgrade it every time,
update, or even start from scratch.

### Advanced Features: Voice, Vision, File Analysis


Now, in this video, let's talk about advanced features, so voice, vision, and file analysis.
So let's start with how CLOT reads documents and images.
So what actually happens in the process once we upload, for example, PDF file, CSV, or
maybe DOCS file?
So firstly, we trigger the text extraction.
The system pulls all the text content from the PDF as plain text.
And as we covered with LLMs, basically, our LLM model can't understand the text, so it
actually needs to break it down into smaller parts, so chunks, and tokenize it.
So that text is tokenized and loaded into CLOT's context window.
Like sending a very long message, CLOT reads it exactly like your chat messages.
To CLOT, it's all just text.
And now, that feature is very useful, because if we have some long text, or even like longer
pieces of the content are usually in the file format, then we can simply just upload it,
and it works simply like as we would do this in a prompt box.
So important limitations.
Scanned PDFs are image files.
CLOT sees a picture, not extractable text.
Very large PDFs may be truncated if they exceed the context window limit.
Tables and complex formatting can lose their structure during extraction.
So this is how our files work inside CLOT, and I would say the most important thing is
that it extracts the text and uses it as the prompt we give in the prompt box.
Now let's talk about images, because it's very important.
How even AI is capable to understand what is on the image and to read it properly.
So we use something which is called a visual encoder, and here it processes the image.
Pixels are covered into a numerical vector data.
Then that data gets translated into representations CLOT's language model can understand.
CLOT exactly reads those vectors the same way it reads any other text in context.
So it's good at text and images, objects, people, layouts, charts, graphs, UI screenshots,
struggles with very small text, handwriting, complex image tables, exact pixel details.
Now let's talk about something which is commonly missed by a lot of people that use CLOT.
And I mean the voice, basically the microphone feature.
Whenever we are, so let's say co-work, chat or code, we've got this small icon to start recording.
And once I do this, CLOT's gonna listen to what I say and just create for me a prompt.
I've got for you also settings, I can basically just choose my microphone or even disable
or enable the hold to record option.
And now once I click on the microphone once again, it's gonna stop it and it's created
for me the entire prompt I can run right now.

### Migrate from ChatGPT to Claude (Import Memory)


Many people move from other LLM models, such as ChatGPT, to Klot,
whether it's from their preference, they want to test out the capabilities,
or overall they want to switch the application.
In this video, I'm going to show you how to migrate from other LLM models to Klot
without losing any data and actually importing all of your important information.
So to begin with, when you're inside Klot, go ahead to the left bottom corner
and click on your profile and then go to Settings
and just click on Capabilities.
And now you're going to have an option, which is Import Memory from Other AI Providers.
You can start an import and what Klot did, they literally created a prompt
that we can use in other LLM models to fetch the data from them
and then feed Klot with that data.
So literally we've got the prompt, we need to copy that
and now we can go to ChatGPT and literally paste the prompt.
However, before you click Enter, I'm going to show you how we can get even more
from this specific thing.
So even more information and enhance the results.
First things first, go ahead to ChatGPT here and click on Thinking Mode.
So it's going to think more complex and it's going to spend more time on thinking
and then it's going to just fetch more results.
Additionally, go to Settings in the left bottom corner here,
then click on Personalization tab and check if you have these three options enabled.
So Reference Saved Memories, Reference Chat History and Reference Record History.
Make sure you have it enabled and with all that being said,
once you do this, you can enter and click literally the prompt
and then ChatGPT creates for me all the information.
So it extracts the data from the application itself
that I can later use inside Klot.
And it's going to extract everything from our personal information,
our career, our preferences about the responses, for example,
our projects and so on and so on.
And once we are done, we can literally copy the thing,
go back to Klot and then just paste the results below to Klot's memory
and we can click on Add to Memory.
And by this way, you can migrate all of your data,
information from any LLM model to Klot literally within minutes.
Just by providing the prompt, running it and then fetching all the information,
pasting it inside Klot and just saving it inside the application.

### Dynamic Prompting


So now I wanna talk about what is a dynamic prompting.
And actually, what is very, very interesting. I'm sure that you have been using this technique
even without knowing what is a dynamic prompting. So there I'm pretty sure that you constantly do
this. Nevertheless, let's cover it. Dynamic prompting is a technique that
involves adjusting the prompt, based on the model's previous responses,
to guide it towards the desired outcome. This iterative process helps refine the
model's understanding and improves the quality of responses by providing feedback
and making adjustments on the fly. And before adding my word, let's cover how it works.
Firstly, you create some prompt. So you start with a simple basic prompt to initiate the task.
Next, you receive the model response. So as always, this is very typical.
And then what happens? You're not satisfied with the response, and what you do in that case?
You adjust the prompt, so you add some additional elements, you delete some of them,
or even you reorganize the entire prompt. So based on the model's response, tweak the prompt
to clarify instructions, provide additional context, or correct any misunderstandings.
And the last step is to repeat the process, making necessary adjustments until the desired
outcome is achieved. So until you get the outcome and very satisfying results.
So again, I'm sure that you've been doing this stuff before. So you create some prompt,
it didn't meet your requirements, and then what you do? You refine it, then you send it to the

Artificial Intelligence is transforming industries by automating tasks, improving efficiency,

that doesn't meet your requirements. So what you do? You adjust the prompt.

and so on and so on. And so you adjust this prompt to get the better responses.
And again, you've got the model response. If it's not that you expected, again,
you repeat this entire process. And lastly, let's take a look at the benefits of this technique.
So improved accuracy. By refining prompt based on the previous responses,
the model can better understand and fulfill the task requirements.

I'm talking about automations, which are deterministic.
So there is no AI reasoning, there is no human in the loop.
And they can break when anything changes.
And the solution is to use skills.
And this is a more flexible approach.
Because you can give LLM or AI agent skills.
And thanks to that, they can adapt to any situation.
You can, of course, implement human in the loop when needed.
Skills can self-improve with every use.
And you can provide thousands per agent by prompting.
So skills are very flexible and powerful.
Now with that context in mind, let's talk about the five design patterns that you can
use when building a skill.
These patterns come from a guide that Anthropic themselves published.
It's a 33-page document all about building skills for Cloud.
I'll put a link to it in the resources section of this lesson.
So you can read the whole thing if you want.
But don't worry, we are going to cover all the most important parts in this section.
So you don't have to read it yourself.
But first, let me quickly remind you how skills actually work behind the scenes.
Because there is something really important that a lot of people don't realize.
When you have skills installed in the project, Cloud doesn't actually load the entire skill
into memory straight away.
Because it uses what's called progressive disclosure.
And it works in three levels.
At level 1, Cloud only looks at the very top of your skill's file, the name and the description. And that's it.
And this tiny little snippet is what sits in context at all times.
It's just enough for Cloud to figure out whether the skill might be relevant to what you are
asking it to do.
And if you run slash context in Cloud Code, you can actually see this for yourself.
All your skills will be listed, but they will only be using a tiny amount of tokens
because Cloud is only holding onto that name and description.
And you will see what I mean in the next lessons.
This is just for the context.
Now if Cloud decides that the skill might be a match for the task at hand, it moves to the level 2.
And this is where it reads through all of the actual instructions in the skill file.
So the step-by-step workflow, the rules, so all of that, all right?
And then it needs even more detail.
So it goes to level 3, which is where it pulls in any linked files like Python scripts, reference
documents or templates.
So you've got three levels.
First, Cloud only looks for the name and the description.
If it decides that the skill is a good match for the task, it moves to the next level.
And it reads through all the instructions included in the skill file.
If it needs more detail, it goes to the next level to use all relevant files like Python
scripts, templates and so on.
And thanks to that, not everything is loaded.
And thanks to that, it can save a lot of context and tokens.
So this is why skills are so efficient.
So this is why skills are so efficient in your context window, because they only load
what's needed and when it's needed.
And it's also why writing a good name and description is so important, because that's
the only thing Cloud sees at first, all right?
So if your description is unclear, Cloud might never get to level 2.
But we'll talk more about that in the next lesson.
So it's Cloud using skills only load what's needed.
And thanks to that, it keeps your context window lean and fast.
Now let's talk about the five design patterns.
And I think the easiest way to think about this is that every skill you create is going
to follow one of those patterns.
So knowing which pattern fits your use case is going to help you design much better skills.
The first pattern is the sequential workflow.
And this is the most straightforward one.
Because it's basically a series of steps that happen one after another in a fixed order. All right?
So the step one has to finish before step two can start, and step two has to finish
before step three can start, and so on.
A good example of this would be a skill that handles user onboarding.
So first you create the account, then you set up the payment details, and then you create
the subscription.
And then you send the welcome email.
And each step depends on the information from the previous step.
First you need the customer ID from step one before you can do anything in step two, right?
And if any step fails, the skill knows to roll back to the previous step.
So this pattern works really well when you've got a clear process with a defined order.
The second pattern is multi MCP coordination.
And this is where things get a bit more interesting because this is for workflows that open across
multiple different services.
So imagine you've got a design review workflow.
In phase one, you use the Figma MCP to pull in the design files.
In phase two, you can use Google Drive MCP to create a project folder and store everything.
In the next step, you use the Linear MCP to create tickets for developers.
And in the last stage, so in phase four, you use the Slack MCP to send a summary to
the team with all the links.
So the skill is orchestrating multiple MCPs in a specific order.
And it can't move from one phase to the next until it has everything it needs from the current phase.
And this is actually a really good example of how skills and MCPs work together.
You can think of it like a kitchen.
The MCPs are like the hands that do the actual cooking.
And they connect Cloud to different services and give it the tools to work with the real data.
And the skills are like the recipes.
They tell Cloud exactly what ingredients to use in what order and in what amounts.
So the MCPs provide the tools and the skills provide the instructions on how to use those tools. All right.
And the third pattern is iterative refinement.
And this is probably one of the most useful patterns for creative work.
And the idea is that you generate something, then you review it, then you improve it.
And you keep going through that loop until you are happy with the result.
So for example, you could have a skill that generates marketing images.
First, it creates an initial version based on your brief, then it spins up a review agent
to check the quality and give feedback, then it refines the image based on that feedback.
And it keeps going through this cycle until the result meets your quality requirements and criteria.
So this pattern is great for anything where you need multiple rounds of improvement.
Things like writing, design, code review, content creation, and so on.
Now the fourth pattern is context-aware branching.
And this one is really clever.
It's where the skills take the same input, but then branches down completely different
paths depending on what that input actually is.
So imagine you have a skill that processes files.
And if you upload a code file, the skill uses the GitHub MCP to create a pull request.
But if you upload a document, the skill uses the Notion MCP to store it and create a summary.
So the skill is smart enough to look at what is being given, figure out what type of things
it is, and then take the right path.
And this is really similar to how automation tools like NA10 or Make work with their branching
workflows, except here it's all defined in a single skill file.
And the last pattern is domain-specific intelligence.
Now this one is more advanced and it's really aimed at larger projects or businesses.
This is where you embed all of your company-specific knowledge, rules, processes into a skill.
So imagine you are working with a company that runs on AWS with a bunch of microservices and databases.
You could create a skill that knows exactly how your infrastructure is set up, which services
are editable, what the deployment process looks like, and what the rules are for making changes.
So whatever the standard operating procedure would be for you to learn how things work
at your company, that's exactly what you would put into this kind of skill.
It's basically an onboarding guide for cloud code that's specific to your business.
So those are the five design patterns, sequential workflow, multi-MCP coordination, iterative
refinement, context-aware branching, and domain-specific intelligence.
And the important thing, you don't have to pick just one.
Some of the best skills actually combine elements from multiple patterns.
But understand these patterns for thinking about how to design your skills.
And I also want to quickly mention the three main categories of skills that you will come across.
The first category is document and asset creation.
What I mean by that, things like generating PDFs, presentations, reports, Excel files, and so on.
The second category is workflow automation.
So where the skill captures a repeatable process that you do over and over again.
And the third category is MCP enhancement.
So where you create a skill specifically to guide cloud how to use a particular MCP server.
And this third category is really underutilized.
Because instead of just letting cloud figure out on its own which MCP tools to call and
in what order, you can create a skill that tells it exactly which tools to use and how to use them.
And this makes the whole process much more readable and predictable.
Now in the next lesson, we are going to zoom in on one of the most important parts of building
a skill, and that's writing good instructions.
Because the difference between a skill that works perfectly every time and a skill that
gives you random results really comes down to how well you have written those instructions.
And of course, I'm going to include all these slides in the resources section of this lesson.
And I will see you in the next one.

Name plus description equals your skill's identity.

Next, multiple variations.

So good output examples.
Make reference file usage obligatory, not optional.

### Testing Your Skills - Three Methods


Alright, so let's say you have built your skill, you have written your description and
your instructions are looking good.
But how do you actually know if the skill works properly?
Because there is a big difference between a skill that looks good on paper and a skill
that actually performs well in practice.
So in this lesson, I'm going to show you three methods to make sure they actually work.
And you can see the three test methods right here on the slide.
Number one is the triggering test.
So does the skill activate when it should?
And the key thing here is to use a new session.
Number two is the functional test.
Is the output correct and consistent?
And for this one, you want to run it four to five times.
And number three is the value test.
Is this skill actually worth having?
And this is about complexity versus benefit.
And it says at the bottom, three tests.
Does it trigger? Does it work? Is it worth it?
So let me go through each of these in more detail.
The first test is the triggering test.
And this is all about checking whether Cloud actually picks up your skill when it should. All right.
And there is a really important detail here.
You need to start a brand new Cloud session for this test.
Don't use an existing conversation, because the context from that conversation might accidentally
nudge Cloud towards using the skill even when it normally wouldn't.
So close your current session, open a fresh one, and then try typing a prompt that should
trigger your skill.
Yes, the second test is the functional test.
And this is where you run the skill four or five times with slightly different inputs
and check if the output is consistent every time.
And basically, if you are getting different results each time, your instructions probably
need to be more specific.
And the third test is the value test, which is simply asking yourself, is this skill actually worth having?
Is it saving me time and improving quality?
Or maybe would it have been easier to just do this without a skill?
So not everything needs to be a skill.
And that's fine.
Now, let's go back to this triggering test.
Because this is where I see the most common issues.
And what we are looking for here is a bit like the Goldilocks principle.
And you can see this on the slide.
So on the left, we've got under-triggering.
That's when the skill is ignored, alright, when it should be used.
And the fix for that is to add more trigger words to your description.
On the right, we've got over-triggering.
And that's when the skill files for everything.
And the fix for that is to make the description more specific.
And in the middle, we've got the sweet spot, which is our goal.
The skill files when it's relevant and stays quiet when it's not.
And as it says at the bottom, test with prompts that should and should not trigger.
And here is a practical example of how to do that.
Let's say you built a skill for cleaning CSV files.
And you can see three test prompts on the slide.
The first one, with the green tick, this should trigger.
So I've got this messy CSV file.
Can you clean it up?
If this key doesn't activate for that, your description probably is not clear enough and
you need to add better trigger words.
The second one, with the red X, this should not trigger.
So can you help me write a Python script?
If this key activates for that, your description is too broad and you're getting over-triggering.
And the third one, with the question mark, this is a great area, I need to work with some data.
And this one might or might not trigger depending on how you've written your description.
And that's fine.
The important thing is that the clear cases work correctly.
So test all three zones, yes, no, and maybe prompts.
Now for the functional test and the value test, I already covered those at the beginning.
But just to quickly add to that, with the functional test, if you are seeing a lot of
variation in the outputs, go back to your skill file and tighten up the instructions.
Add more detail about what the output should look like.
Specify the format more clearly or add an example of the expected output.
You can also try running the skill with sub-agents or in an agent team setup to see if the behavior changes.
With the value test, just be honest with yourself.
So not everything needs to be a skill.
If the task is simple enough that you can just ask Cloud directly each time, or if it
would be better as a simple Python script.
So don't force it into a skill.
And one more thing that applies to all three tests, iteration.
So the first version of your skill is almost never going to be perfect.
So we need to keep that in mind.
And that's completely fine because after each round of testing, go back to your skill file,
make the improvements and test again.
And very useful tip is you can actually tell Cloud about the issue and ask it to help you fix the skill.
So for example, you could say something like, the skill is triggering when I ask about Python
scripts, but it shouldn't be.
Can you tighten up the description?
And Cloud will update this skill file for you.
Now one more thing before we wrap up, as you can see on the slide, the path from project
level to global skill.
So it goes in three stages.
Stage one is project level.
So basically use the skill in a single project to test it daily.
Stage two is battle test.
So we run it for weeks in your real workflows.
And stage three is global skill.
So you promote it when it consistently delivers good results.
And this is very important.
Don't make a skill global until you have used it in your real workflow for at least a few
weeks and it consistently delivers good results.
So first project, then battle test, and then global.
So it has to earn the promotion.
And battle testing doesn't mean using it a couple of times, you know, over an afternoon.
It means running it for a few weeks in your actual workflow.
And once you are confident that it works well, then you can, then you can graduate it to
a global skill that you can use across all your projects.
But please, until then, keep it at the project level so you can continue tweaking it without
affecting anything else.
So to recap, test one is the triggering test, where you check that the skill activates at
the right times.
Test two is the functional test, where you check that the output is correct and consistent.
And test three is the value test, where you check that the skill is actually worth having.
So run all three of these on every skill you create.
Decide based on what you find, and you'll end up with skills that work reliably every single time.

### Creating a Skill Manually from Scratch


Alright, so in the previous lessons we covered all the theory, design patterns, good vs bad
instructions, testing and folder structure.
Now it's time to actually put all of that into practice.

And you can see this on the slide. So this table.
If the process is wrong, like the skill skips steps or does them in the wrong order, then
we update the skill.md instructions.
If the skill needs more info, like more context about your brand or your audience, then we
add a reference file.
If the skill does something it shouldn't, then we add a rule and we update the skill.md
add a rule to the rules section.
And if the skill struggles with an MCP or a tool, then we guide it manually first and
then create an MCP reference doc so it knows how to do it next time.
And at the bottom, we can see something really powerful.
Self-improving skills.
You can actually instruct the skill to save approved outputs as good examples in the references
folder automatically.
And you can also tell it to add corrections to the rules, to the rules section, whenever
you point out something that shouldn't ever happen.
So the skill gets better every single time you use it.
And without you having to do manually, go in and edit the files yourself.
Now, let me talk about three specific editing scenarios that come up a lot.
And you can see them right here on the slide.
The first one is to rigid.
So this is when you have defined the output format so strictly that Cloud can't adapt
to different situations.
And the fix is to loosen the instructions slightly to allow user preferences instead of hard rules.
And the second one is to loose.
This is when the instructions are so general that Cloud produces different results every time.
And the fix is the opposite.
Add more steps, more examples, and clear output format.
And the third one is too big.
So this is when the skill is trying to do too many things at once.
And the fix is to split it into two focus skills.
For example, maybe our eBay skill is trying to handle both, drafting new emails and replying
to existing threads.
And the instructions for each are so different that they're getting each other's way.
In that case, you'd create a second skill folder, move the relevant instructions over
and update the descriptions so each skill triggers for the right use case.
So to sum up, there are three ways to edit your skills.
You can edit the files directly in Visual Studio Code or any other IDE where you use
Cloud Codes or directly in Cloud Desktop App if you are using it.
And this method gives you the most control.
You can also ask Cloud Codes to make the changes for you, which is great when you know
what you want, but not exactly how to implement it.
Or you can use Cloud Cowork on the Desktop App for a more conversational approach.
So whichever method you choose, always test after each change.
And please don't be afraid to keep refining your skills over time.
The best skills are the ones that evolve with your workflow.


### How to Edit and Improve Your Skills


Alright, so we have built our skill, we have tested it and it's working.
But the chances are after using it a few times, you are going to change something.
Maybe the output format is not quite right, maybe you want to add a new feature, or maybe
you have noticed that the skill triggers in situations where it shouldn't.
So in this lesson I'm going to show you how to edit your skills and there are a few different
ways to do it, depending on what you need to change.
Now the most direct way to edit a skill is to just open up the files and change them
directly, alright?
And this is the approach I would recommend for most edits because you have full control
of exactly what changes.
So let's say you want to edit the image draft writer skill we built in the earlier lesson.
All you have to do is go to the file explorer in Visual Studio Code, navigate to our skill
and open up the skill.md file.
Now the skill.md file has a few different sections that you might want to edit.
So let me walk you through each one and explain what you can change and why.
So the first section is the YAML front matter at the very top.
So the name and description.
Now you probably won't need to change the name very often, but the description is something
you might tweak quite a lot.
So remember, this is the part that determines when Cloud triggers your skill.
So if you are finding that the skill is not triggering when it should, you probably need
to add more trigger words to the description.
Or if it's triggering too often for things it shouldn't handle, you need to make the
description more specific.
And this is the most common edit you'll make and can have a huge impact on how well your skill performs.
The second section is the instructions.
So the step-by-step workflow.
And there are all sorts of reasons you might want to change this.
Maybe you want to add a new step to the process.
Maybe you want to change the order of steps.
Maybe one of the steps is too vague and you want to make it more specific.
Or maybe you have been using the skill for a while and you have realized there is a better
way to do something.
Whatever the reason, just open the file, find the section you want to change, and edit it directly.
It's just a markdown file, so there is nothing complicated about it.
And the third section is the references.
If you want to add new reference material, like additional examples, updated templates,
or some guidelines, you just create new files in the references folder, and then add a reference
to them in your skill.md file.
So for our email skill, maybe you want to add a new tone, like Argent.
We would need to update the ToneGuide.md file in the references folder, and then add a mention
of it in the instructions.
And the fourth section is the scripts folder.
If your skill has one.
If you need to change how a Python script works, maybe you want to add a new feature or fix a bug.
You just open the script file and edit it like any other code file.
In this example, of course, we don't have the scripts folder.
So that's the manual approach.
Open the files, make your changes, save them, and test.
So nice and simple.
The second way to edit a skill is to ask Cloud Code to do it for you.
This is really useful when you know what you want to change, but you are not sure exactly
how to implement it.
So for example, let's say we want to add a new feature to our email draft writer skill.
You want it to be able to handle email chains, where it reads the previous emails in a thread
and drafts a contextual reply.
So instead of figuring out the exact instructions ourselves, we can just tell Cloud Code what we want. So let's try.
I want to update the email draft writer skill, please add a new section to the instructions
for handling email chains.
When the user provides previous emails in a thread, the skill should read through the
whole chain, understand the context, and draft a reply that's consistent with the conversation so far.
Make sure it picks up the tone of the previous emails and matches it. And send it.
And Cloud is going to open up the skill.md file, find the right place to add this new
section, and write the instructions for us.
As you can see, yes, I want to confirm.
Do you want to make this edit to skill.md?
Yes, I approve it.
The skill now includes a dedicated handling email chains section.
Alright, perfect.
So then we can review the changes and tweak anything we don't like.
Now, the third way to edit a skill is something that Christian already touched on in the basics
section, and that's using Cloud Cowork on the desktop app.
And this approach works really well if you are not comfortable editing files directly.
What you do is give Cloud Cowork access to the folder where your skill lives, and then
you just chat with it about what you want to change.
So you could say something like, the email skill is being too formal.
Can you make it more friendly, professional? Whatever.
And Cloud Cowork will go into the files, make the changes, and you can review them.
Now regardless of which method you use to edit your skill, there is one really important
thing to remember.
And that's to test after every change.
Don't make five changes at once and then test.
Make one change, test it, make sure it works, and then make the next change.
This way, if something breaks, you know exactly which change caused the problem.
And here is another useful tip for editing skills over time.
Keep a mental note or even a small look of the changes you make, because skills evolve
as your workflow evolves.
So the first version of a skill is almost never the final version.
You will keep refining it as you use it more and discover edge cases that you didn't think of initially.
And that's completely normal, it's actually a sign that you are using the skill properly.


### Exercise Create Your Own Skill


Alright, so this is your exercise for this section.
You have now got all the knowledge you need to build a skill from scratch.
You know the design patterns, you know how to write good instructions, you know how to
structure your folders and you have seen a complete skill built step by step.
Now in this exercise, I want you to create a skill that solves a problem you actually
have in your day to day work.
Now you can actually build this using the skill creator that Christian showed you in
the previous section, or you can use cloud on desktop app.
So just give it access to a folder and ask it to build the skill for you.
Either way works great for this exercise.
So here are a few ideas to get you thinking if you are not sure what to build.
You could create a meeting notes skill that takes rough notes from a meeting and turns
them into a structured summary with action items and follow ups.
You could build a social media caption writer that generates kind of platform specific captions
with the right hashtags and tone for your brand, or you could create a data report generator.
So this skill could take raw numbers and generates a weekly or monthly report in a specific template.
Whatever you choose, here is what I want you to do.
So please find a real problem from your workflow and first decide which design pattern fits your use case.
Is it sequential, iterative, or branching?
Or maybe something else?
Second, create the folder structure with skill.md file and a references folder at minimum.
Then please write the YAML frontmatter with a specific description and clear trigger words.
Fourth, write structured step-by-step instructions, not vague paragraphs.
And finally, add at least one reference file, either an example output, a template, or maybe
a guidelines document.
So once you have built your skill, try breaking it on purpose.
For example, you can change the description to something vague and check if it still triggers.
You can change the instructions to be less specific and see how the output changes.
And this will really help you understand what makes a skill actually work.
And of course, in the next sections, once you learn the basics of Cloud Code, you'll
also learn how to build more advanced skills using Cloud Code and all the powerful features
we showed you in this section.
So there is a lot more to come.
Good luck and I'll see you in the next section.
